With the list, the loader requires every selected module that requires or prefers `cap:lake-query` and every selected agent that needs it to be listed, and refuses a reader listed twice or a writer below `red`. `hnx modules render` writes `lake-access.json`, the query engine's file-based access rules: catalogs (the lake catalog only, every other `none`), schemas (no owner), tables (each reader's `information_schema`, then every table with the row filter `coalesce(tlp, 'red') IN (...)` below `red`, none at `red`, insert and delete but never update for writers) and queries; rules run users before groups, each from the lowest ceiling up, so the most restrictive applies; an unlisted principal reads nothing (`homelabnext.modules.lake_access`). `homelabnext.lake.ceiling.CeilingReader` is the shared read path for code here: it builds its own filtered `SELECT`, checks each row's label again and refuses raw SQL below `red`. `tlp.at_or_below` and `tlp.row_filter_sql` name the labels and the predicate. The full example site declares its readers; three selection negatives.
- The log pipeline sets each event's TLP label (ADR-0033 decisions 3 and 5; docs/log-pipeline.md, "The TLP label"): `homelabnext.logpipeline.enrich_tlp(event, route, table)` writes `labels.hnx_tlp` from the dataset the collector says the event's input feeds (`<module>/<dataset>`, beside the event, never read from it) through `tlp.item_label`: an item label in the event's `tlp` field, the flat `"labels.tlp"` key or `labels.tlp` (the strictest of several, read from the event as it arrived) is kept when stricter and, when looser, only where the dataset honours item labels; one that is not a label is `red`; an unknown, missing or malformed route is `red`; every spelling of a self-set `hnx_tlp` is replaced. `SourceTable` (`tlp_sources/v1`) is the table, and `hnx modules render` writes it as `tlp-sources.json` from the site's selection (`homelabnext.modules.tlp_sources`). The `log-pipeline` contract gains `load_sources` and `Observed.route`; its suite gains seven MUST checks (twenty-four, the ceiling) and eight mutants (twenty-one), each caught by its own check alone; `other-fields-unchanged` leaves the TLP label to them.
- The lake stores each row's TLP label (ADR-0033 decision 5; schemas/ocsf/README.md, homelabnext/lake/README.md): the five core OCSF tables are 1.1 (additive, frozen beside 1.0) with a top-level `tlp` column limited to the five canonical labels. It labels the row, not OCSF's `osint.tlp`. The column is optional in the schema, so rows written before it stay valid, and every write must carry it: `normalize_rows` refuses a batch with any row whose label is missing or not canonical, in every adapter. Lake string columns honour a JSON Schema `enum`; `TableSchema.labelled` and `tables.LABEL_COLUMN` name the column, and `tables.row_label` reads a stored row's label (`red` unless it is a canonical spelling: an alternate one such as `white` was stored around the lake). A `str` subclass cannot pass the enum check, and an enum the lake cannot enforce (empty, not a list, on list items) is refused. The DDL, Arrow and Iceberg goldens gain the column; the synthetic fixtures carry every label; each table has a `tlp-not-a-label` negative. A live table needs the column added and its old rows backfilled, a later step; until then a swap cannot shadow-start from a lake whose rows predate the column.
- Datasets declare their TLP label (ADR-0033 decision 3; docs/modules.md, "`data:` and lifecycle safety"): the draft module schema gains `data[].tlp` (one of the five labels, canonical spelling; absent is `red`) and `data[].honour_item_labels` (a boolean that needs `tlp` beside it: an item may keep a looser label of its own only when it is `true`). `homelabnext.tlp.item_label(declared, item, honour_item_labels=...)` is one item's label (no label field: the declaration; a `null` label is `red`; a stricter one is kept; a looser one is raised to the declaration unless honoured; an unreadable one is `red`), and `homelabnext.modules.rules.dataset_label` reads a dataset's declaration. Both fields sit under `data`, so any change to them is human-approve, and `homelabnext/tlp.py` joins the review tier's GOVERNANCE list. Four schema negatives: an unknown label, a non-canonical spelling, a non-boolean `honour_item_labels` and `honour_item_labels` without `tlp`.
- The TLP label type (ADR-0033 decision 1; docs/modules.md, "TLP labels"): `homelabnext.tlp` with `Label` (an `Enum` that compares only with another `Label` and is always truthy; `clear` < `green` < `amber` < `amber+strict` < `red`), `LABELS`, `read()` for labels on data (an accepted spelling, else `red`; never raises), `parse()` for declarations (raises on anything else), `strictest()` (derived data; `red` for an unreadable input, a non-iterable or none; a single string is one input) and `allows(ceiling, label)` (a parsed ceiling only). Accepted spellings are the five labels, optionally `tlp:`-prefixed, in any ASCII case, with surrounding spaces, tabs, CR and LF ignored, and `white` as `clear`; `amber-strict`, `tlp: red` and non-ASCII look-alikes are not labels, and a `str` subclass cannot change how it is read. The publishing sidecar's enum is held equal to `LABELS`, and the module is under the security coverage floor.
- ADR-0033: data carries a TLP 2.0 label from its source and every reader has a TLP ceiling. Unlabelled data, and the lab's own data, is `red`; sources declare their label from the provider's terms; derived data takes its strictest input, and only a reviewed release transform labels output looser; storage refuses an unlabelled write; displays, exports, agent profiles, MCP servers and model roles each declare a ceiling, filtered in the shared read path; roles that leave the lab default to `clear` and never receive `amber` or above; `data_tier` is derived from the ceiling; egress points are enumerated; secrets are never labelled data; `green` is not public. Free, open enrichment lookups with no sharing restriction are `clear` (decision 3), so a public threat-intel rollup of `clear` inputs is `clear`. External enrichment lookups are egress (decision 13): a job takes the strictest label of the records that cause it (a lab sighting is `red` even when the value is also in a `clear` feed), bounded by the submitter's ceiling; each external analyzer declares a maximum, enforced by the platform's per-analyzer maximum TLP or in the submission path, and one with no maximum is sent nothing.
- `hnx modules swap-gate --overlay DIR` runs the swap gate over a private overlay's own `lab/` files (`lab/model_roles.json`, `lab/model-serving/model_store.json`, `lab/model_roles.eval.json`, `lab/model-evals/`, `lab/swaps/`) with the repository's guarantees (evidence from the base commit, the head table through the symlink-checked reader, fail closed; the golden suites are the installed package's), and the overlay template's `checks.yml` gains a `swap-gate` job that runs it on every pull request (docs/modules.md, docs/private-overlay.md).
- Site schema 1.6 adds `router.wan6`, the router's own IPv6 on its WAN (docs/network-model.md, "The WAN's IPv6 (1.6)"): exactly one of `static: {address, gateway}` or `ra: true`. `hnx site validate` requires a static address canonical, with no zone id, a /64 of global unicast (never unique-local, documentation, link-local, special-purpose or IPv4-embedding), not an anycast address of the /64 and outside every zone's `subnet6` and `delegated_prefixes6` prefix, and a gateway that is link-local or another usable address in the WAN /64. `linux` renders it as a drop-in for the WAN's own networkd `.network` file (`<wan_network>.d/50-hnx-wan6.conf`; the new option `wan_network`, default `10-<wan_interface>.network`) or an ifupdown `inet6` stanza (`interfaces.d/hnx-wan6`): `IPv6AcceptRA=yes` (SLAAC only, no DHCPv6, `UseOnLinkPrefix=no`) or `inet6 auto` for `ra`, an address and a default route via the gateway for `static`. `default.accept_ra` and `default.accept_redirects` are 0, the WAN takes no redirect or route-information option, and its `accept_ra` is set after `all`'s (2 for `ra`, 0 for `static`); every zone takes no advertisement (networkd: `IPv6AcceptRA=no` on a zone without `subnet6` too). nftables takes a router advertisement on the WAN only (link-local source, hop limit 255), in the input chain and the reverse-path exemption, and the RFC 4890 ICMPv6 input whenever the router has any IPv6; the forward chain drops IPv6 to a zone's prefix leaving by the WAN, before `established,related`, and an output chain does for the router's own, so a prefix advertised on the WAN cannot pull a zone's traffic out; NAT is unchanged. Prefix delegation is not expressed (ifupdown has no clean form). Other adapters ignore `wan6`, and validation (or `render`/`plan --adapter`) warns that they do. A site without `wan6` renders byte for byte as before, with the same IR digest.
- The module selection names the lab's own lake namespaces (`lab_table_namespaces: [{namespace, why}]`, `why` required; docs/modules.md, "Site selection" and "Lake tables, from the catalog"). With the list, `hnx modules check --running` reports a gathered table in a namespace that no module id binds, that is not the core's and that is not listed (`tables X runs in namespace N, which is no module's, the core's or the lab's`); `[]` says the lab keeps none. Without the list such a table is still not judged, and the check now notes that the lab's namespaces are not declared. The loader refuses a listed namespace that is listed twice, the core's (`ocsf`) or any module id's (`-` read as `_`). `hnx site review-tier` rates a namespace added, or the list dropped, human-approve, and a namespace removed, a `why` reworded or the list declared where it was absent owner. The full example profile lists two namespaces; two new selection negatives cover a missing `why` and a namespace of the wrong shape.
- The module census gathers the lake tables (ADR-0027 follow-up 9; docs/modules.md, "Lake tables, from the catalog"). On the host that runs the lake catalog, `module_census_catalog_url` (plain http on a loopback IP literal), `module_census_catalog_warehouse` and, optionally, `module_census_catalog_api_root` and `module_census_catalog_token_file` (a plain name in `/etc/homelabnext`, only with the URL) make the role run `hnx modules catalog-tables` there: the Iceberg REST catalog's top-level namespaces and their tables, listed with GET requests only, no redirect followed and no proxy, each page read to at most 1 MiB, more than 4,096 namespaces or tables a failure, and two minutes for the whole listing. The token file is read on the catalog host in one step (no link, a regular file with one link, the reader's own, no group or other access, one line of token characters), the task becomes root only to read it, and the token is never printed: an error names the request and its HTTP status, never the catalog's words, and only names reach the runner. Names not of the inventory's shape are left out and counted, as are namespaces nested in a listed one (counted, never listed) and a namespace gone before its tables were listed. The loader judges only `ocsf.*` and module-id namespaces, and the selection's `lab_table_namespaces` the rest (below). `hnx modules gather` reads the names strictly and fills `tables` (the union over every catalog listed; `not_gathered` when none was). The loader now judges a table in the core's namespace that is not one of the core's OCSF tables, and notes when the core's tables were not read. `RestCatalog` takes an `opener`, lists namespaces (`list_namespaces`, under a `parent` when given), caps both listings with `limit`, refuses a listing entry of another shape as a `LakeError`, and gives each HTTP error a `bare` text without the server's words.

- The module census gathers the MCP servers the installed clients register (ADR-0027 follow-up 9; docs/modules.md, "MCP servers, from the installed clients"). A host's `module_census_mcp_configs` lists its installed client configurations (`{path, client}`: `claude-code`, `generic`, `hermes`: a fleet host's clients), trusted overlay input whose files must be named as their client's configuration (`.mcp.json`, `<name>.mcp.json`, `mcp.json`, Hermes `config.yaml`). The role reads each in one step (`library/mcp_client_configs.py`: no link at any step; every directory and the file root's or the connection user's; no directory its group or others may write except a root-owned sticky one; a regular file with one link, at most 1 MiB, no blocking) and parses it on the host, so a configuration's text, which can hold secrets, never leaves it: the answer carries per file whether it is present and the server names it registers, and both tasks run with `no_log`. JSON files refuse duplicate keys, `NaN` and `Infinity`; Hermes files are read with PyYAML's safe loader with aliases and duplicate keys refused, and fail the census on a host without PyYAML. `hnx modules gather` checks the names again and fills `mcp_servers` (an absent file registers nothing; a name that is not an inventory name, or a file that does not parse, fails the census). The loader matches a registered name back to its catalog record by the record's instance template (`homelabnext.modules.loader.instance_pattern`): a name bound to a module only against that module's records, an exact id first, then the template fixing the longest prefix, a name several records still match reported as ambiguous; a template that begins with a placeholder other than `{id}` is refused (`template_problem`). A name bound to no module must be one of the core's MCP records when the core's catalog was read (`CoreNames.mcp_instances`, `is_mcp`). The `llm-orchestrator` client's catalog is not read.
- The module census reports the sockets each host listens on, and the loader judges each selected module's service row by them (ADR-0027 follow-up 9; docs/modules.md, "Service rows, by what listens"). The `module_census` role's new `listening_sockets` module reads the kernel's socket tables (`/proc/net/tcp`, `tcp6`, `udp`, `udp6`, each to a fixed size; a role var, `module_census_socket_tables`, so group_vars cannot repoint it) and each answer carries `listening`: every listening TCP and bound, unconnected UDP port as `tcp/<port>` or `udp/<port>`, with `@lo` for one bound only to loopback (a line that does not parse is skipped; more than 16,384 distinct sockets fails the census). `hnx modules gather` parses them strictly (at most 16,384 per host) into the draft `platform/module_inventory`'s new optional `listening` (per host). Rows stay in `not_gathered`, since a socket names no row; with `listening`, `hnx modules check --running` reports a selected module's row as declared on its placed host (a provider's per instance, on that entry's own placement; the census host of that name, or whose first DNS label it is, compared case-insensitively) but not listened on from an address other hosts can reach (TCP; UDP for `udp`; either for `dns`; a loopback-only socket answers no other host), and notes a row whose host it cannot name. The comparison is derived and one way: a port another program holds (a client's UDP socket included) reads as the row answering, a row with no socket on the host (NAT into another network namespace with no proxy process) reads as not answering, and an undeclared row is never seen.
- The module census gathers the running notifier's recovery pairs (ADR-0027 follow-up 9; docs/modules.md, "Notify pairs"). A process that owns a notifier (`hnx broker serve`, `hnx status collect --notify-topic`) given `--notify-pairs-dir DIR` (`HNX_NOTIFY_PAIRS_DIR`) writes the pairs it holds to `DIR/broker/pairs.json` or `DIR/status/pairs.json` (`homelabnext.notify.record`, schema version 2): when built and after each registration, the whole set each time, with when it was written, the host's boot id and the writer's pid and start time, mode `0644` (it discloses which pairs and module ids run on the host to its local users). `DIR` and `DIR/<owner>` are opened once with no link at any step and must be owned by root or the writer and writable by no one else; the lock (on the owner's directory itself), the temp file and the replace are relative to that open directory, so a planted link, a FIFO or a path swapped after start cannot redirect or block a write. No role here deploys the broker or the status hub, so the operator makes the directories (owners and modes in the docs). The first write, at start, raises; a later one that fails is logged, the pair is still held, and the record is written again at the next registration or report. `Notifier.register_pair` takes an optional `module` (the module a pair was loaded from; never guessed) and refuses the same title for another module; nothing in the core passes `module` yet, so a module's running pair is reported as no module's until a module loader does. Role `module_census` reads each owner's record at the fixed `/var/lib/homelabnext/notify-pairs` (`module_census_pairs_dir`) in one step with its `notify_pairs_records` module (no link at any step, directories writable by their owner only, a regular file of at most 256 KiB, a FIFO never blocks), with the host's boot id, clock and the start time of each record's process. `hnx modules gather` parses each strictly and counts only a record that still says what runs: not one from before the host's last boot, a broker record only while its process runs, a status record (one pass per timer run) only for 900 s; each other is noted. With no counted record the pairs are left out and noted as not compared. The loader now also judges a running pair bound to no module: it must be one of the core's own (`homelabnext.notify.core_pairs`) with the same recovery, or it runs but is no module's or the core's; without the core's pairs it says the pair was not judged. A record is the owning service's word about its own pairs, believed as a host's `systemctl` output is. `homelabnext.schemas.module_manifest.TITLE_RE` is the notify title pattern compiled.
- The module census gathers the agents the running broker serves (docs/modules.md, "Agents, from the running broker"; docs/broker.md). The broker gains an **operator** role and its one operation, `agents` (`hnx broker agents --socket PATH`, `BrokerClient.agents`): broker manifest 1.8 adds `operators` (`[{uid | user}]`, empty by default; a peer is listed once across all five roles), and no peer is an operator unless listed there, root included (an unlisted uid 0 is `unknown-caller`, as before). The answer is the agent keys whose profile the manifest both maps a transport peer to and grants something, names only, audited as the new operation `agents` (audit 1.4; the record names no key), and so is each refusal; an agent, a relay, the runner and a sandbox are refused it (`not-the-operator`), an operator is refused every other operation, and `hnx broker agents` names the `operators` key when refused. Role `module_census` asks it on a host whose inventory sets `module_census_broker_socket` (a path under `/run/`; with `module_census_hnx_cli`, an absolute path, default `/opt/hnx/bin/hnx`), from that one task as root (`become`), which the broker host's manifest lists as an operator, and writes the answer's lines as the host answer's `agents`; `hnx modules gather` parses each line as one agent key (anything else fails the census), fills the inventory's `agents` with the union over the brokers asked, and leaves `agents` in `not_gathered` when no host's census asked one.
- `hnx site review-tier --base REV | --ci-base [--root DIR] [--site REL] [--selection REL] [--json]` (`homelabnext.network.review_tier`): the review tier of a change to what the lab claims, against a base read through git (one resolved commit, `--no-replace-objects`, capped regular blobs; the head read like every loader input). The default is human-approve: a change is owner only where the check knows it widens nothing. Judged by their own rules, a narrowing being owner: in the site, a delegated IPv6 prefix added or changed, a zone added, a zone's `subnet` or `subnet6` changed or added, its egress opened (`internet` anything but `deny`, `one_way` off, any other key), a firewall `allow` added or changed or a `deny` removed or changed, the service exceptions reordered (first match wins), any `tailnet` change; in the module selection, an address added to `scope.trusted` or `scope.target`, an address removed from `scope.exclude` or a part declared where it was absent, any grant added. Owner because they widen nothing: `site_version`, `site`, `edge`, `power`, a zone's `purpose`, `tags` and `dhcp.lease_hours`, a selection host's `capacity`. Every other changed key is human-approve with its path (a zone's `vlan_id`, `gateway`, `dns`, `dhcp.range`; the `router`, `switches`, `dns`, `dhcp_reservations`, `adapters`; the selection's `profile`, `hosts` addresses and `isolated`, `slots`, `features`, `core`, `providers` and placements). Every change to the registry (`lab/services.yml`), to `lab/guests/`, `lab/modules/` and `lab/modules.lock` is human-approve as a whole (`hnx modules review-tier` reads only the repository's module roots). A new, removed, unreadable or sealed file (a top-level `sops` key), a mapping key that is not text, and a shape it cannot compare are human-approve (fail closed). Like `hnx modules review-tier` it exits 1 only when a human-approve change has no valid `CODEOWNERS` owner as of the base: the approval itself is the pull request's Code Owners review, which binds only where the branch rules require it. The private-overlay template's `checks.yml` runs it on every pull request (job `site-review-tier`: the pinned `hnx`, a full-history checkout, no secret), its `CODEOWNERS` names `lab/modules.yml`, `lab/services.yml`, `lab/guests/`, `lab/modules/` and `lab/modules.lock` beside `lab/site.yml`, and every template job that checks out the pinned `hnx` into `hnx/` first fails when the overlay itself carries an `hnx/`. `homelabnext/network/review_tier.py` joins the module review tier's governance files; `swap_evidence.changed_paths` lists what a working tree changes under given paths.
- The network side renders a zone's `subnet6` (site 1.4). The IR carries it with the router's address, the prefix's `::1` (`Interface.subnet6`, `Interface.address6`, `Policy.zones6`, `router_address6`), and every adapter renders each policy rule's IPv6 form (`ipv6_form`) right after the rule. A rule between zones has one when every zone it names has a prefix. An allow naming a host has none (hosts are IPv4-only, so IPv6 to them stays closed), while a deny naming a host denies the same traffic to the host's whole zone, so a later, wider allow cannot pass in IPv6 what the deny refuses in IPv4. An internet rule has one only from a routed prefix. `linux` renders the router's IPv6 address and router advertisements (networkd's sender, or `etc/radvd.conf` for `flavor: ifupdown`), IPv6 forwarding with `accept_ra = 0`, an `ipv6_addr` set per zone, ICMPv6 and management SSH to the router, and a strict IPv6 reverse-path check (`fib saddr . iif oif missing drop`; IPv6 has no `rp_filter`, and without it a host could claim another zone's source). `unifi` renders the networks' static IPv6 settings and the policy in `LANv6_IN`/`WANv6_OUT` inside the same allow-established, drop-invalid and default-deny frame as IPv4, so IPv6 between zones is never left to the controller's default. `dnsmasq` and `zonefile` are unchanged (no DHCPv6, no AAAA). A site without prefixes renders byte for byte as before, and its IR digest is unchanged (the IPv6 fields are left out of an IPv4-only site's IR). The router's IPv6 input takes only the ICMPv6 RFC 4890 requires (echo rate-limited), and the reverse-path check's exemption is on-link control traffic only (neighbour discovery at hop limit 255 and MLD at hop limit 1, from `::` or a link-local source). The controller's IPv6 rules use the IPv4 index range (2000 to 2999) and `protocol_v6`; that range, the field and the controller's own `LANv6_LOCAL` and anti-spoofing defaults are checked on the server. A host deny before a zone allow makes IPv6 stricter than IPv4 (the deny closes the host's whole zone): `docs/network-model.md` says so. The WAN's own IPv6 is `router.wan6` (site 1.6). A guest's IPv6 extra address may no longer be its zone's `::1`, in compute validation and the `hnx_address` filter alike.
- A site may declare the global-unicast IPv6 prefixes it owns (`delegated_prefixes6`, `platform/site` 1.5, additive): a list of `{prefix, why}`, the reason required. In the lab-only judgement any destination network inside one is the lab's; a guest's `outbound_allow` names IPv6 only through a zone, so in practice a zone's global `subnet6` inside a declared prefix, which a model runtime (a VM, a container or a physical host) may then allow through `zone:`; outside every one it still reaches the internet. `hnx site validate` requires each prefix canonical, a /48 to a /64 (what an ISP delegates to one site; a /32 would claim an ISP's whole allocation), global unicast outside `2001::/23`, the retired 6bone `3ffe::/16`, the reserved `2000::/16` and documentation space, with no zone id and nothing IPv4-embedding, and no two overlapping (`delegated_prefixes6`, `delegated_prefix6_problem`); the compute path judges the list again, since it reads the site file unvalidated, and a declaration that does not read is a site problem that holds nothing as the lab's. The zone-prefix and delegated-prefix rules share one checker (`_prefix6_problem`). Rendering, a zone's own prefix rules and guests' IPv6 extra addresses are unchanged. `hnx guests` and the topology map pass the declaration through (`site_delegated6`, `load_fleet(delegated6=)`). The declaration is the site's word, not a proof, and its `why` is a note, not a control: the private overlay's `CODEOWNERS` template now names `lab/site.yml`, which gates the edit only where the overlay's branch rules require an approving Code Owners review; without that rule it is unreviewed site configuration (a human-approve check on changes to `delegated_prefixes6` is a follow-up). A zone's `subnet6` and the `hnx_address` filter's prefix rule now also refuse the retired 6bone `3ffe::/16` and the reserved `2000::/16`.
- The module loader at deploy time and the inventory gathered from the hosts (ADR-0027 follow-up 9; docs/modules.md, "The loader at deploy time"). `hnx modules check --deploy` is the deploy's check: it needs `--overlay`, refuses `--examples`, and a lab input the overlay lacks (the selection `lab/modules.yml`, the role table, the core registry) is a problem instead of a fallback to the repository's examples. The private overlay's deploy workflow always runs it before anything changes on a host (a problem stops the run), and its `checks.yml` runs it on every pull request with the pinned `hnx` and no secret. A lab with none of `lab/modules.yml`, `lab/modules/` and `lab/modules.lock` (a dangling link counts as present) is checked as an empty selection, never skipped: the inputs that only judge selected modules are notes, and every running unit of a module is one the site does not select; with modules selected and no review policy, the check notes that every fetched module is refused. After the deploy the workflow always takes a census of what runs: role `module_census` (`ansible/playbooks/module_census.yml`, read-only, per host one `systemctl list-unit-files --state=enabled 'hnx-*'` and one `systemctl list-units --state=active 'hnx-*'`, with `systemctl` pinned on the command line, the answers written on the controller; a host name that is not a plain name is refused), `hnx modules gather DIR --out FILE` (`homelabnext.modules.census`: every fleet unit some host reports enabled or active, each line parsed strictly; a host that did not answer, an entry that is no listed host's answer, a link or an oversized file fails the gather; the census is a union across hosts, so a unit is not checked against the host it runs on), and `hnx modules check --deploy --running FILE`, a verification that fails the run after the deploy has applied. The gatherer binds nothing: a unit is a module's by its name, as before. The draft `platform/module_inventory` 1.0 gains `not_gathered`, naming the kinds an inventory does not report (a gathered one: service rows, MCP servers, agents and tables), which the loader notes as not compared; a kind left out without it is still refused. The core's own fleet units now have a source: the loader reads them from what installs them (every unit file a core role's tasks install through a `dest:`, families with one unit per instance included, and the units the egress and mirror generators write, each generator's `UNIT`), never from a list; a running `hnx-*` unit that is neither a module's nor the core's is a problem ("runs but is no module's or the core's"), an empty selection included, and a module unit named like a core unit is refused. A test scans every role file and the package for a unit name the loader does not read. Without `ansible/roles` under `--root` the core's units are not read: a note, and under `--deploy` a `registry` problem. The census sees only `hnx-*` services and timers, and it runs with the overlay's own Ansible configuration and inventory, so it is an alarm against drift, not a gate against a hostile overlay (which has root through `site.yml`); the docs say both. The loader has not run at a real lab's deploy yet, so ADR-0027 stays proposed.
- The module loader compares recovery pairs and the core's own MCP ids, agent keys and tables (ADR-0027 follow-up 9). The draft `platform/module_inventory` 1.0 gains an optional `notify_pairs` (the running notifier's `{problem, recovery, module?}`; no `module` is the core's): with it, a running pair is declared by its module, which the site selects, with the same recovery title, every selected module's pair runs, and a title runs once; without it the report notes that pairs were not compared. Per site, a problem title is declared once across the selected modules and its recovery is another title (`pair_problems`). The uniqueness check against the core's names now also refuses a module MCP server id from the core MCP catalog beside the registry (`mcp/`), an agent key the generated keyring holds as an agent principal (`hnx modules check --keyring`, else `lab/keyring/keyring.json`, else the example's), a table among the core's OCSF tables, a module whose table namespace is the core's (`homelabnext.lake.tables.CORE_NAMESPACE`, `ocsf`), and a module notify pair whose problem or recovery title is one of the core's own. The core's pairs are now written once, in `homelabnext.notify.core_pairs` (`CORE_PAIRS`), which the broker, the status hub, the power monitor, the model-call budget and the serving-config check register from; a test fails on a registration that writes a title in place. `Notifier.register_pair` refuses to re-register a problem title with another recovery (it kept the last one). A recovery title names one thing: it is not another pair's problem or recovery title. A notify title, in the manifest and the inventory alike, refuses controls, no-break and other spaces, and zero-width and bidirectional marks. With `--overlay`, the MCP catalog (`lab/mcp/`) and the keyring (`lab/keyring/keyring.json`) are the overlay's: a missing one is a `registry` problem unless `--mcp-catalog` or `--keyring` names it, never the repository's example; without an overlay a missing source is a note, and with `--json` the notes also go to stderr. An MCP record's id is its `id`, else its file's stem, as the catalog's loader takes it; a record-like file that is not `<id>.yml`, an id that is not its file name and a catalog over 1,000 entries are `registry` problems; the OCSF tables are read through the loader's capped reader. The core's own units were not compared yet (see the deploy-time entry above). The example small-lab inventory lists its notify pairs.
- The network sensor's rows carry the zone's IPv6 prefix: a central control's `<role>_guests` row for a guest with extra addresses now carries its zone's `subnet6` (site 1.4) after `subnet` when the zone gives one, and `hnx_enroll_address_problems` holds an IPv6 extra address to it, as guest validation does, so `suricata_enroll` no longer refuses a global IPv6 extra address validation accepted. The filter checks `subnet6` by the site's own prefix rules (canonical, a /48 to a /64, unique-local or global unicast less 2001::/23, never IPv4 written inside IPv6), refuses an IPv6 extra at the subnet-router or an RFC 2526 anycast address of its /64, holds no IPv6 extra to an unusable `subnet6` (or any extra to an unusable `subnet`), and, without `subnet6`, keeps accepting only unique-local and documentation IPv6 addresses.
- A guest's extra addresses (`network.extra_addresses`, guest 1.6) now reach the network sensor and the topology map. A central control's `<role>_guests` row carries `extra_addresses: [...]` after `ip`, and the zone's IPv4 `subnet` after `zone`, when the guest names any (rows of guests that name none are unchanged). `suricata_enroll` puts every address of every enrolled guest in `HOME_NET` and in its zone's `HNX_ZONE_<ZONE>`, IPv6 included, so traffic from a second NIC or a container's bridge address is no longer `EXTERNAL_NET`. Because `HOME_NET` is what the sensor trusts as the lab's own, the role first holds every address to the guest model's rules with a new filter, `hnx_enroll_address_problems` (`ansible/plugins/filter/hnx_address.py`; `ansible.cfg` gains `filter_plugins`, as does the private overlay's), which parses each address and names every bad one: canonical, never loopback, link-local, multicast, reserved, a cloud metadata address or IPv4 written inside IPv6; `ip` IPv4 and inside the row's `subnet`; an extra address inside the row's zone `subnet` (never its network or broadcast address) or the shared range a tailnet uses, and, in a hand-written row with no `subnet`, private, shared or documentation space only; IPv6 extras unique-local or documentation; a `subnet` a canonical /8 to /30 holding the guest's `ip`, no wider than /24 where it reaches public space. The topology map resolves a host, firewall source or destination at any of a guest's addresses, in any spelling, to that guest (a single-address CIDR, /32 or /128), and its card and table list the extra addresses apart from the address; the dataflow page's host line under a guest shows all its addresses. Where two guests claim one address, which a valid site never has, the map now keeps the first guest (a primary address always before any extra), where it kept the last. The vuln-scan and backup-verify roles still enroll `ip` only.
- A zone's IPv6 prefix (`platform/site` 1.4, additive): `subnet6`, a /48 to a /64, optional. `hnx site validate` requires it canonical (lower case, compressed, no host bits) and refuses a link-local, multicast, loopback or unspecified prefix, one that writes IPv4 inside IPv6 (mapped, compatible, translated, NAT64, 6to4, Teredo), and two zones' prefixes that overlap. A guest's IPv6 `network.extra_addresses` is held against it: inside its own zone's `subnet6` and never inside another zone's, so a global IPv6 address (a SLAAC one included) can now be listed and is ownership-checked; an anycast address of its /64 (the subnet-router one, and the top 128 interface identifiers RFC 2526 reserves) is refused. A prefix must come from unique-local, global unicast (less the IETF protocol assignments `2001::/23`) or documentation space. Where the guest's zone has no `subnet6`, the 1.6 rule stands (a global IPv6 address is refused; a unique-local or documentation one is accepted with no ownership check), now also refused when another zone's prefix holds it; a `subnet6` that `open_site` reads without `hnx site validate` is held to the same rule, and when any zone's `subnet` or given `subnet6` does not read (an empty or non-text one included) or the guest's zone is not in the site model, every IPv6 extra address is refused. `ZoneInfo` gains `subnet6`. The example zone `compute` has `2001:db8:0:82::/64`, so `infer-core`'s IPv6 extra address is ownership-checked. Nothing else is configured from it: the adapters remain IPv4-only (`zone:` references in guest firewall rules and egress validation: see Fixed).
- A guest's other addresses (`platform/guest` 1.6, additive): `network.extra_addresses` lists what else a guest sends from besides `network.ip` (a second NIC, a macvlan or bridge address its containers use, a unique-local IPv6 address), each a bare address with a `why`, at most sixteen. The `runtime-labels` render labels every one of them like `network.ip`, so a model runtime sending from its second address is still labelled `model` and the three model-runtime detections still fire (new evasion fixtures: a runtime calling itself `none` from its extra IPv6 address, the same address written uncompressed and upper-case, and its extra IPv4 address seen as a dual-stack peer; a test records the limit that a peer seen through NAT64 is not found). Validation holds each to an address that names this guest and no other wherever a collector sees it: canonical, with no IPv4 written inside IPv6; not loopback, link-local, multicast, reserved or a cloud metadata address; in IPv4, inside the guest's own zone subnet (or a zone-less overlay range, a tailnet's) and never inside another zone's; in IPv6, inside the guest's own zone's `subnet6` (or, without one, a unique-local or documentation address); not a zone's gateway, network or broadcast address, a gateway a guest names, or inside a DHCP pool; not an address the site's static DNS names for something else; and not one any guest already names; named once, and not the guest's own `ip`. Where the guest's zone gives no IPv6 prefix (`subnet6`, site 1.4, above), a global or SLAAC IPv6 address is refused. `ZoneLookup` now requires `all()` (every zone), and a lookup that cannot list a zone a guest is in is a problem when any guest names an extra address; `load_fleet` takes `site_addresses` (`homelabnext.compute.zones.site_addresses`, the site's static DNS `A` records), which `hnx guests` passes. The scope guard resolves every address of a guest, and every spelling of an address (IPv6 written long, IPv4 inside IPv6 including NAT64's well-known prefix, hex, octal, integer and short dotted IPv4) to one canonical form; such a spelling that resolves to no target is denied even under `unknown: allow`. The example model runtime `infer-core` names an IPv4 and an IPv6 extra address. Nothing is configured from them: addresses that change (DHCP leases, IPv6 privacy addresses) or that several guests share (a NAT, a proxy) still cannot be labelled.
- `hnx modules swap-check RECORD --site FILE --base REV | --ci-base` (and `make swap-check`) and `hnx modules swap-gate --base REV | --ci-base` (and `make swap-gate`): a swap record 1.2 checked with its model evidence read from the repository rather than supplied by the caller (`homelabnext.modules.swap_evidence.from_git`), and **everything that vouches for the new model read from the base commit, never from the change**: the role table before, the model store, the evaluation reports (`examples/model_roles.eval.json` and every report under `examples/model-evals/`, at most 64) and the golden suites. Only the table after is the working tree's, read like every loader input (no symbolic link on its path, the root included; capped; non-blocking). So a model is vetted in one change (its store entry and its live report, which `hnx modules review-tier` now rates human-approve) and rebound in a later one; a change cannot vouch for itself. The base is resolved once to a commit (`rev-parse --verify --end-of-options REV^{commit}`): an option-like value or a tree is refused. Live evidence is policy; `--report` is for local runs only. `swap-check` refuses `--overlay` (the evidence is read at the repository's fixed paths). `swap-gate` is the CI gate: when the role table differs from the base's, a swap record added or changed under `examples/swaps/` must cover it, its legs holding against the base's evidence; an unchanged table passes. A model-role leg's new binding must be on the instance the leg moves (`svc:<instance>`, the row the swap renames the incoming provider's to, inferred from the name): a rebind or an added fallback onto any other backend is refused. The `--ci-base` rule moved into the package (`homelabnext.gitbase.ci_base`; `tools/schema_check.py` re-exports it), so no CLI loads a file from the checkout to find its base, and CI's new `trusted-gates` job runs `review-tier` and `swap-gate` with the base commit's own code (a detached worktree, imported with `python -P`), reading the change only as data. What is still trusted: a report's `live` (a reviewed claim now, not a proven one), and this workflow file and the dependency constraints, which a pull request run takes from the change and which are human-approve. A rebind within one provider module has no record of its own, so `swap-gate` needs a swap record for any change to the example role table.
- The log-pipeline slot contract and the runtime label (docs/log-pipeline.md), which arm the three model-runtime detections. `hnx guests render --target runtime-labels` writes `runtime-labels.json` (`homelabnext.logpipeline.runtime_labels/v1`), mapping every guest's address to `model` (a model runtime, the same test validation uses) or `none`; the example lab's table is committed at `examples/log-pipeline/` and re-rendered by `hnx guests plan --exit-code` in `make gates` and CI. `homelabnext.logpipeline.enrich` is the rule: drop any label the event arrived with, the flat `"labels.hnx_runtime"` key included (the engine reads it first) and any spelling of either that differs only in case or white space, join on the address the collector saw (never `source.ip`, `host.*` or anything else the event says), and set the table's value; an unknown address, no address, or one with a port, zone id or name gives no label, and a record that is not an object passes through unchanged. An unknown address and `none` are the same to the detections, so a runtime seen from an address the table lacks is silent; providers should count unlabelled events (a follow-up, as is loading the table at deploy time). The slot registry gives `log-pipeline` the contract `log_pipeline.py`, an extended conformance suite (`hnx conform run log-pipeline`) of seventeen MUST checks with thirteen mutants, each caught by its named check alone, and the example provider is no longer provisional. End-to-end evasion fixtures (`tests/logpipeline/evasion/`) run the enrichment and then the rule: a runtime calling itself `none`, setting the flat key, spelling it in another case, naming another source or arriving as an IPv4-mapped peer still fires; another guest or an unknown source calling itself `model`, a guest that runs no model sending a PUT to a bucket, or an event with no observed address, stays silent. `det:model-runtime-egress-drop`, `det:model-runtime-object-store-lookup` and `-request` move from `unsupported` to `experimental`, so they are counted in `detections/attack_coverage.json` (T1567.002) and compiled into the engine bundle. Both object-store rules match the bare path-style endpoints `storage.googleapis.com` and `storage.cloud.google.com`, not only their subdomains. On a pass-through `log-pipeline` (single-box, small-lab) nothing sets the label and they stay silent.
- The model-role leg of swap record 1.2 (docs/modules.md, "Swap record 1.2"): the draft `platform/module_swap` 1.0 gains an optional `model_roles` list (additive), with an example and six negatives. Each leg names a role, the old and new binding (by pin digest or model-store entry) and the digest of the evaluation report for the role table after the rebind (`homelabnext.golden.pins.report_digest`). `homelabnext.modules.swap.model_role_problems` checks the legs against both tables, the reports and the store. Every role whose bindings differ between the tables has exactly one leg and no other role has one. Both bindings resolve and differ, and the rest of the role is unchanged. The report passes `hnx golden pins check` for the new table, the new binding's canary entry included. Live is the checker's policy (`ModelEvidence.require_live`, default on, which also requires the store): a leg may ask for it and never waive it, and with the policy off the new binding's own entry must still be live unless the table after is the shipped example. `module_swap_problems`, `cutover_problems` and `apply_selection_diff` take that evidence as `models=` and refuse a swap of the `inference` slot without it, legs or none. A malformed leg is refused by name. A report's `live` is its claim, checked for shape and pins. Calibration refit has no field in the record and is not enforced; the 1.1 swap kit carries no legs.
- The egress canary (docs/compute-model.md, "The egress canary"): `homelabnext.golden.canary` and `hnx golden canary` pose tasks a model can answer from the prompt alone. The tasks are detection rules modelled on the October 2026 report, a log summary, a plain question, and two tasks prefilled with the reported steering triggers. The model gets a shell and a fetch tool the harness never runs, with N reproducible samples per task and a bounded `max_tokens`. A sample fails on any of: an unneeded tool call; a host or address outside the task's allowlist (numeric and IPv4-mapped forms normalised); a cloud metadata address or name; a public object-store domain; a write verb; a resolver read; a `/tmp` listing; a reply that does not parse, whose raw text is scanned; or an inconclusive prefilled, empty or truncated reply. The pinned evaluation (G-11, `hnx golden pins check`) now canaries every binding of every role (eval version 2). Each entry records its pin, role params and whether it ran live or against the fake, and the check validates it against the current suite and binding, recomputing its totals. `--require-live` (`MODELS_CHECK_FLAGS`) refuses a binding no real canary has run against, and without it `pins check` still fails a role table other than the shipped example whose canaries are all fake; the committed report is the fake's and passes by construction. `make models-canary` runs the canary live. It samples behaviour and proves nothing: the default-deny egress of model runtimes stays the control.
- The per-field review tier for in-tree modules (ADR-0027 follow-up 4; docs/modules.md, "Trust tiers"): `homelabnext.modules.review_tier` and `hnx modules review-tier --base REV | --ci-base` classify every changed path under `modules/` and `examples/modules/` as human-approve (anything the Trust-tiers table gives an approver other than the owner: credentials, control-plane readers, egress, data, lifecycle, interfaces, agents and growing data grants, roles, entry points, fleet units, service rows, MCP records, shared config, hooks, workflow packs, a privileged slot provided, required, preferred or fallen back to, the module's identity; the files these name in the base manifest, interface schemas and a privileged provider's conformance target among them; a new file the base names no directory of; any file nothing names) or owner, the head's manifest only ever adding human-rated names, failing closed (new, removed, renamed, unreadable or linked paths, undefined keys, unkeyable lists, no slot registry). The schema, the slot registry (both ends) and CODEOWNERS are read as of the base, and a change to them, to the classifier, to `tools/schema_check.py` or to `.github/workflows/ci.yml` is human-approve; CODEOWNERS is matched by GitHub's rules with owner tokens validated. The command fails when a human-approve change has no valid code owner, whose pull-request review is the approval; with today's `*` owner line that is near-vacuous. It runs in the CI `schemas` job (`--ci-base`); `make modules-review-tier` runs it locally against `origin/main`, outside `make gates`. `.github/CODEOWNERS` names `/modules/` and `/examples/modules/`.
- The module loader (ADR-0027 follow-up 9; docs/modules.md, "What the loader checks"): `homelabnext.modules.loader` and `hnx modules check|render`. It discovers manifests under `modules/`, the example fixtures (`--examples`) and an overlay's `lab/modules/`, refusing a manifest that does not parse, is not its directory's id, takes a reserved id or shadows a shipped one; validates every manifest and the selection against their schemas before any rule; runs the per-manifest, cross-manifest and site rules with every input built from the contributed files, the supply chain for the selected modules, and uniqueness against the core's reserved ids and registry service rows; checks contributed files (regular, inside the module, no symbolic link, valid record types); and, with a hand-written inventory (`platform/module_inventory` 1.0, a draft schema), compares what runs with what the selected modules declare. Every file is read without blocking, as a regular file of at most 1 MB, once per run, within a per-module (2 MB) and per-run (8 MB) budget, YAML with duplicate keys and aliases refused and deep nesting refused, and a symbolic-link module directory or manifest and a hard-linked manifest or contributed file are refused; each is a named problem, never a hang or an internal error, and untrusted names are printed escaped. A provider instance may not take a core service name of another capability. What it did not check (no site, no inventory, the contributed kinds with no record type, an input taken from the examples) is printed as a note. `render` writes the footprint limits and egress policy for a site that checks clean, into a new or empty directory. It runs over the example lab in `make gates` (`make modules-check`) and CI; nothing runs it at deploy time yet.
- Model runtimes get default-deny egress (docs/compute-model.md, "Model runtimes"), after Cohere's October 2026 report of an open-weight model that tried to upload a file to a public cloud bucket while asked to write a detection rule. Validation refuses a guest whose capability or inventory groups name `cap:inference` or `cap:codemode-executor` unless its `policy_out` is DROP or REJECT and no `outbound_allow` reaches the internet (`any`, a `/0`, or a CIDR outside the never-internet ranges), or, with its NIC firewall off, its exemption names the layer that filters it (`filtered_by`, guest 1.5: a guest that filters, or a zone whose site-model egress denies the internet) and a `review_by` date that has not passed (`HNX_TODAY` overrides today); every guest's `filtered_by` must resolve to a guest that filters (its baselines resolve `host-firewall` or `egress-filter` and its `policy_out` is not ACCEPT; not exempted or retired, so no chains; a guest's own NIC firewall filters only itself) or a zone, its `review_by` must be a real date, and no `outbound_allow` may hold a metadata address; a model runtime's `zone:` destinations are judged by their subnet; with a baseline set that defines `egress-filter` it must also resolve that control. The example set gains a `model-runtime` baseline and the example lab a model runtime, `infer-core`, that uses it. Where `nftables_host` runs (a guest whose baselines resolve `host-firewall` or `egress-filter`), it now drops the cloud instance metadata addresses first, whatever the policy and ahead of established flows, in its output chain and in a new forward chain ahead of a container engine's (container bridges), logging `hnx-egress-refused: `, and logs what default-deny drops as `hnx-egress-drop: ` (both rate-limited); module egress refuses a metadata address as a host's address. Five detections: `det:cloud-metadata-probe` and `det:cloud-metadata-refused` (T1552.005), and `det:model-runtime-egress-drop`, `det:model-runtime-object-store-lookup` and `det:model-runtime-object-store-request` (T1567.002), which stay inert (`status: unsupported`) until the pipeline labels a model runtime's events (`labels.hnx_runtime`); the detection tooling now honours `status`: an unsupported or deprecated rule runs its fixtures but is neither counted as ATT&CK coverage nor compiled into an engine bundle, and lint refuses a status outside Sigma's.
- The remaining trust-tier grants (ADR-0027 follow-up 4; docs/modules.md, "Trust tiers"): site rules, not module rules, now decide what only the overlay can grant. A unit that runs as root or opts out of hardening needs `grants.units`; edge or idp shared config needs `grants.shared_config`; a service row whose upstream is the broker, idp or remote-admin slot (or an instance filling one, or a preferred slot whose fallback is one) needs the new `grants.upstreams`; a required model role whose prompts can leave the lab needs the new `grants.cloud_models`. Every grant is refused as stale when it names nothing a selected module uses. `platform/model_roles` 1.3 adds `leaves_lab` on a binding, additively, followed down a role's fallback chain, and a contributed agent's model role must be one its module requires. `site_problems` takes the rows, units, root units and role table as required inputs, so no rule is skipped for want of one. The per-field review tier for in-tree manifests is still open.
- The product-name lint (ADR-0027 rule 1 and follow-up 10; docs/modules.md, "The product-name lint"): `tools/product_name_lint.py` fails when a product's name (a provider's name, display name or alias, a product in `tools/product_names.yml`, a model id of the role table) appears in the core or a feature or core module, outside provider modules, site configuration, the role table and prose. It reads the files `git ls-files` lists: Python literals and identifiers (not comments or docstrings), YAML and JSON keys and values (not prose keys), every other text file's non-comment lines, and file and directory names, case-insensitively, camel-case seams included. A provider module's directory may name only its own product. Today's names are counted in `tools/product_names.baseline.json`; `make product-names-baseline` rewrites it and is refused when a product's total would rise, and CI's `--ci-base` refuses a baseline or vocabulary loosened since the base commit. It runs in `make gates`, the CI `schemas` job and as invariant X-11 (checklist pillar X, now 102 PUBLIC-NOW rows).
- `platform/interface` 1.0, a draft schema (ADR-0027 follow-up 3; docs/modules.md, "Interfaces: one owner"): the shape of a manifest `interfaces:` entry, generated from the module manifest's definition (it changes with it until acceptance), with the examples' two gate interfaces as its examples; no loader reads a standalone record yet. Gate ownership: a gate module marks its consuming `requires` or `prefers` entry and its definition `gate: true`, and the module and cross-manifest rules refuse a gate on an interface another module defines, a gated interface whose definition is not marked, a marked definition its owner does not gate on, a gate interface its owner also provides, and an id marked on some versions only (per id, every version). An interface nobody marks as a gate is not covered. `publishable-output` and `sample-handoff` are marked.
- The supply chain of fetched modules (ADR-0027 follow-up 5; docs/modules.md, "Supply chain"): `homelabnext.modules.supply` computes a module's tree digest (a versioned header and every file's path, executable bit and content; a link, special or hard-linked file, `.git` entry or a tree over 20 000 files or 2 GiB refused), and `lock_problems` holds every selected fetched module to the overlay's `lab/modules.lock` (draft schema `platform/module_lock` 1.0): pinned, its origin the lock's, its directory still the pinned digest, and its reviews (in the lock, never in the module, in the shape of `homelabnext.reviews`, naming the full digest) meeting the review policy's new `module-provider`, `module-feature` and `module-core` kinds, with any non-pass verdict a veto, the author never their own reviewer and never `unknown`, and a module of an unknown kind, or of a kind the policy leaves out, never loadable. In-tree is decided by where a module is, never by its manifest. `hnx reviews digest <module dir>` prints the tree digest; the example policy asks an agent check, an independent agent audit and a human approve of every module kind. A policy may leave the module kinds out (it then loads no fetched module), so existing policies still load.
- The egress generator (ADR-0027 follow-up 6; docs/modules.md, "Egress and publishing"): `homelabnext.modules.egress` renders, per lab host, an nftables ruleset (`table inet hnx_egress`) in which a socket in a module role's slice may open connections only to DNS (port 53 unless the provider has rows), the slots its module requires or prefers (a fallback only when the preferred slot is absent; the provider's rows at their ports, or the provider's hosts), its `svc:` targets and, when declared, global unicast addresses outside the lab except DNS over TLS and over QUIC (port 853), so lookups stay on the lab's resolvers (every special-purpose range, `scope.trusted.nets` and every host's address excluded, merged so no set element overlaps); the rest is logged and dropped, as is any other socket in `hnx.slice`, and an isolated role reaches nothing. `hnx-egress.service` loads it only on cgroup v2 with every role slice present, before `network-pre.target`, the module's fleet units start after it, and `hnx-egress.timer` reloads it every 30 seconds. The site selection's hosts gain `addresses` (draft `platform/module_selection`, one address each); the example sites carry documentation-range ones. Every address, port, protocol and name is checked before it reaches the ruleset. What it does not cover (inbound, loopback, containers with their own network namespace, root processes, established flows, DNS as a channel) is documented.
- Import validation for stateful slots (ADR-0027 follow-up 7; docs/modules.md, "Import validation"): `homelabnext.modules.importcheck` holds an imported tree to its export manifest (the draft schema `platform/export_manifest` 1.0: slot, format, every file's path, size and sha256, totals; `manifest_sha256` is its canonical digest) and refuses traversal, non-NFC paths and case-folding collisions, symbolic links and special files anywhere (the root included), files with a second hard link, entries on another filesystem and mount points inside the tree (failing closed on an unreadable mount table), missing and unlisted files, size and digest mismatches and trees over the importer's limits or the caller's tighter ones (`Limits.tighten`), naming up to 100 problems at once and never quoting content; a hostile manifest is refused, never a crash. Everything goes through one `O_NOFOLLOW` descriptor of the root and re-checks each file on its open descriptor, so a file or directory swapped for a link after the check is refused, not followed; `copy_tree` streams a verified tree into a new directory, flushed and renamed into place only when complete. The new extended conformance suite `migration` states the MUST (refusal before any change included); five deliberately broken importers, one defect each, are each caught by exactly the check that names it. No provider proves it yet: no slot protocol has `export` and `import_`, so the suite runs the reference importer labelled as each stateful slot, and a per-slot table records what proves each.
- Footprints as limits (ADR-0027 follow-up 8; docs/modules.md, "Footprint and resources"): `homelabnext.modules.limits` renders, per lab host a site selection places modules on, a systemd slice per module host role (`hnx-<id_>-<role_>.slice`: `CPUQuota` from `cpu` times `count`, `MemoryMax` from `memory_mb` times `count`, `MemoryHigh` at 90 %, `MemorySwapMax=0`), drop-ins that run each module's fleet units (`hnx-<name>.service`, bound to the module id) in its slice, and `hnx-module-quota.sh`, which caps and reserves each module's data root by a ZFS `refquota` and `refreservation` or an allocated ext4 image mount that keeps the root's owner and mode, never mounting over existing files or a foreign mount, recovering what a failed run left and carrying on past a module that fails (`--check` changes nothing and exits 3 while a cap is owed). Every footprint value is checked before it reaches a unit file or the script. A role's own services join their slice with `Slice=`; GPU memory stays a placement sum; roles on external hosts are listed, not rendered. Tested over the three example sites, and the script against stubbed tools.
- A `prune-branches` workflow (run by hand from the Actions tab, with a `dry_run` input) deletes the branches whose work is over: only a branch whose tip is the head of a merged or closed pull request from this repository and that no open pull request uses; the default branch, protected branches, branches with commits after their pull request closed and branches that never had one are kept. `tools/prune_branches.py` decides (with tests), and the run summary lists what was deleted and what was kept, and why.
- `hnx secrets plan|seed` (docs/private-overlay.md, "Seeding the credentials"): `plan` lists every `secret:<name>` the overlay and the roles name, and how each gets its value under `lab/secrets.yml` (template: `examples/private-overlay/lab/secrets.yml`, classifying every name the roles use; kinds `password`, `token` with an optional prefix, `hex`, `external`; a name no rule matches is `unclassified` and never generated). `seed` creates each missing value in a KV version 2 mount with check-and-set 0 (an existing or deleted credential is never overwritten; a version 1 mount is refused), asks before writing (`--yes`), takes external values with `--ask` or `--from-file NAME=FILE`, exits 3 while any value is still owed, and with `--vaultwarden` copies each into a Vaultwarden folder through the Bitwarden CLI (the value on its standard input, `bw` without the OpenBao token). Output names secrets, never values.
- A four-box lab example (`examples/four-box-lab/`, docs/four-box-lab.md): one large hypervisor with capacity, pools and a guest mirror; a physical sensor and DFIR server on the switch's uplink mirror and the hypervisor's mirror; a detonation box in the one-way zone; and a secondary DNS on a single-board computer. It validates and renders every network adapter and guest target (`tests/integration/test_four_box_lab.py`), and the page says what to replace, in order, to make it a real site.
- `platform/module_swap` 1.0, a draft schema (`x-draft`, ADR-0027 proposed; docs/modules.md, "Swap record 1.2"): what swap record 1.2 adds, keyed on modules and slot instances, with `legs`, `route.selection_diff` and `dependants.report` defined. `homelabnext.modules.swap` recomputes the dependants report against the incoming provider (core modules included; a failed requirement blocks, a failed preference falls back) and refuses a record whose report, selection diff, legs, migration format or count, or revoked credentials do not hold, and applies a selection diff as the cutover. The documented `samples` object-store swap runs through it end to end in `tests/schemas/test_module_swap.py`. `platform/swap_record` stays 1.1.
- Node mirror (docs/compute-model.md, "Node mirror"): a node in `_nodes.yml` may declare `mirror: {to: <nic>, bridges: [...]}`, and the new `hnx guests render --target node-mirror` writes, per such node, `hnx-mirror.sh` (a `tc` matchall mirror on the ingress of every guest port of the bridges, kept current by a 15-second systemd timer; `--remove` undoes it) with its service and timer. It shows a network sensor the guest-to-guest traffic a switch mirror cannot see. Interface names are checked; the script keeps correct filters on a re-run (a matchall filter cannot be replaced in place), reports a port that fails and mirrors the rest, refuses a missing, bridged or addressed capture NIC and a missing bridge before changing anything, and silences the capture NIC; the unit runs with only `CAP_NET_ADMIN`.
- Mirror ports (site schema 1.3, additive, frozen): `port_assignments` takes `kind: mirror` with `mirror_from` (the source ports), a SPAN destination for a network sensor on its own machine. `hnx site validate` requires `mirror_from` on a mirror port and refuses it elsewhere, refuses a VLAN on a mirror port, a source that is unassigned, listed twice, a mirror port or already mirrored, and on a `unifi` switch more than one source or destination per entry, and warns when a mirror copies a management or one-way zone; the UniFi dry run renders a port override with `op_mode: mirror` and `mirror_port_idx` (`docs/network-model.md` "Mirror ports").
- Node capacity and storage pools in `_nodes.yml` (docs/compute-model.md, "Node capacity and pools"): `capacity: {threads, memory_mb, reserve: {threads, memory_mb}, cpu_overcommit}` and `pools: {name: {size_gb, media}}`. `hnx guests validate` refuses a node whose guests (every one not retired) ask for more cores than its threads after the reserve times the overcommit ratio, more memory than its reserve leaves, a disk on a pool it does not have or more than a pool's size, and prints each node's use. Optional: a node without them asks nothing. The lab-topology example declares both.
- `platform/slot` 1.0, a draft schema (`x-draft`, ADR-0027 proposed; docs/modules.md, "The slot registry"): the slot registry's fields `contract` (a protocol file, or null for a provisional slot), `stage` (1-10 or null), `formats` (`in`, `out`), `stateful` and `export_formats`, `required_in_core`, `privileged` and `pass_through`, with their rules: export formats exactly when stateful, a privileged slot has a contract, a pass-through slot has a stage, passes something (non-empty formats in and out) and is not required in core. Every entry of `examples/modules/slots.yml` validates against it (replacing three hand-written asserts), and the privileged set is pinned to the six documented trust roots, each with a conformance suite. `platform/capability` stays 1.0: its rules on these fields would not be additive over an open 1.0 object, so descriptor 1.1 takes them when the ADR is accepted.
- The gateway spec's `telemetry_reader:` (`{key: secret:...}`) generates the orchestration telemetry's spend-log reader, which the operator used to create by hand (delete a hand-made one: it is not found by alias): `litellm/keys.json` gains a `users` entry (`hnx-telemetry-reader-<name>`, `proxy_admin_viewer`, which reads every spend-log row, `models: ["no-default-models"]`) and a `telemetry-reader` key owned by it with `allowed_routes: ["/spend/logs/v2"]`, the lock LiteLLM 1.103.3 checks for every role (its viewer role does not refuse inference, and the model block stops only requests naming a model). `hnx gen gateway provision` refuses a keys file that grants more than a spec declares (`grant_problems`: another user shape; `key_type`, `team_id`, `permissions` or an every-model list on any key; a reader key with other fields) before any request, then creates or corrects the user before its key (`/user/info`, `/user/new` with `auto_create_key: false`, `/user/update`) and prunes the key when the reader leaves the spec; the keys audit applies the same rules. The orchestrator example declares one.
- The gateway spec's `controller:` block takes `base_url` (required: `https://HOST[:PORT][/PATH]` with an ASCII host name, no label that reads as a number, and no credentials, escapes, whitespace, query or fragment, the forms on which URL parsers disagree about the host), and `hnx gen gateway render --check` (`make gateway-check`) also fails when the home of a controller backend has a `model.base_url` that is not that URL in that same strict shape (scheme and host without case, the default port and one trailing `/` ignored), when its role has no `max_context_tokens` (this used to pass silently when the backend had no `max_context` either) or its `model.context_length` is set and not a whole number of tokens up to its role's `max_context_tokens` (`homelabnext/generators/agent_homes.py`; the two follow-ups docs/orchestration-controller.md left open after A-14). The fail fixture gains a home that names its engine and one whose context outgrows its role.
- Optional features of the `orchestration` contract (clauses f-j of `conformance/protocols/orchestration.py`, ADR-0001 additive; `docs/orchestration-controller.md` "Additive `orchestration` contract features"), with their checks as the extended suite `orchestration-features` (the `orchestration` suite would reach the 24-check ceiling with them): `priority` (`submit(..., priority=)`, 1 first; SHOULD `lower-priority-number-starts-first`, which needs `admission` and a reported limit), `shadow` (`submit(..., shadow=True)`: every attempt on the shadow lane, one unfinished shadow run per job, `ShadowRunPending` otherwise; `shadow-run-executes-only-on-the-shadow-lane`, `one-unfinished-shadow-run-per-job`), `constraints` (`define(..., constraints={require_tags, min_context, exclusive})`, `ConstraintsUnsatisfiable`; `unsatisfiable-constraints-never-run`, `every-attempt-meets-the-constraints`, `exclusive-run-has-its-backend-alone`), `local-only` (`define(..., local_only=True)`; `local-only-run-never-reruns-on-cloud`) and `list-runs` (`runs(state=)`; `runs-lists-every-run-once-in-its-state`), observed through `backends()` and `placements(run_id)` (`Backend`, `Placement`). The in-memory fake implements them all: it hands a freed slot to the waited-on queued run with the lowest priority, and places runs on two local backends and a cloud one. Seven MUST checks, each with a mutant caught by it alone (`orchestration-features` joins `ONLY_ITS_OWN`); the priority check's defect is pinned by `tests/conform/test_conform_orchestration_features.py`. The `llm_orchestrator` adapter declares `priority` only (the job API's 1..100); its check skips there because the controller reports no limit, and a test drives the recorded stand-in at concurrency 1 to show the lower number starting first.
- `hnx gen gateway render --check` reads the orchestration controller's backend list when the gateway spec has a `controller:` block (the controller's config and one `HERMES_HOME` per local backend): it fails when a backend `name` is not a `svc:` of the spec, when its home's `model.default` is not a role bound to that backend, or when its `max_context` differs from the role's `max_context_tokens` (`homelabnext/generators/agent_homes.py`); the role must be bound to that backend alone, and the controller config and homes must lie inside the spec's directory. The worked example is `examples/llm-orchestrator-gateway/` (fictional: spec, role table, controller excerpt, two homes, generated gateway config), checked by `make gateway-check`.
- Invariant A-14, "No agent home names an IP address or an engine model id" (checklist row and fast criterion in `examples/registers/invariants.yml`): `hnx gen gateway homes --roles ROLES [--store STORE] HOME...` fails on a `HERMES_HOME/config.yaml` naming an IPv4 or IPv6 address (documentation ranges included; the gate `tools/no_raw_endpoints.py`'s detection plus `ipaddress`) or an engine model id (a role-table binding's `model_id`, or a store entry's `served_model_name` or hub repository, in a value, never a key, without case, alone, behind a provider prefix or with a `:tag`/`@quant` suffix; never a role alias); a home that is not UTF-8 or not YAML is could-not-measure (exit 100). The gate is loaded by one shared helper, `homelabnext/endpoint_gate.py`, which the customization lint uses too. Pass and fail fixtures in `tests/generators/fixtures/hermes-homes/`.
- `hnx docs index generate|check [FILE]`: the description after each link in `docs/README.md` is generated from the linked page's front-matter `summary:` (a `<!-- hnx:gen:summary:PATH -->...<!-- /hnx:gen -->` span per entry; sections, order and links stay authored), so the index cannot drift from the pages. `check` fails on a stale span or a target without a summary and runs in `make gates` and CI.
- Summary freshness (ADR-0032): a missing summary is now a failure of `hnx registers summaries` (it was a warning), and every authored docs-page summary carries `summary_of: outline:sha256:<16 hex>`, a digest of the page's heading outline (`levels.outline_digest`). The lint fails a page whose outline changed or that has no stamp (body edits that touch no heading do not); `summaries --restamp PATH...` rewrites only that line after a reread (`--restamp-all` is for a migration only, and the old `--missing` flag is gone; a symbolic link, a `..` path and a register record are refused, writes are atomic and all-or-nothing across paths). Generated summaries and register records are exempt; `summary_of` is a machine key of the system-page review digest. All 69 authored pages are stamped.
- Every docs page carries an L0 `summary:` (ADR-0032 levels; a missing summary now fails the lint, see the summary freshness entry above): authored ones on the 73 hand-written pages and ADRs, generated ones (`summary_generated: true`, through the shared `levels.generated_summary_lines`) on the scripts catalog, the status sources page, the terms page and the strategy index. `hnx registers summaries` reports 0 pages without one. The ADR log test reads an ADR past its front matter.
- `hnx conform mutants --all` runs the extended suites' mutants as well as the core ones (`mutants all` stays the core suites, as `hnx conform list --all` adds the extended ones to the list); every mutant of every suite is caught. `docs/codemode.md` records how the operator-gated CM-13 and CM-14 probes are to be built: an extended suite `codemode-executor-isolation`, proved with `mutants --all`, judged by what harness-owned listeners received, with undecidable probes untested rather than passed.
- Read actions for the Code Mode correlation (CM-12 of `docs/codemode.md`): the example broker manifests gain `dns.lookup` (a service's name and addresses), `dhcp.leases` (a DHCP server's leases) and `hypervisor.guests` (a node's guests with their MAC and state), read tier, each about a required target, with stand-in handlers (catalogued scripts), handler documents and `result_schema`s; fictional data only (names under `lab.example`, RFC 5737 addresses, RFC 7042 documentation MACs). Only `profile:triage` is granted them (read tier, through the broker; the least the golden task needs), and the production example manifest does not carry the stand-ins. The Code Mode outputs are regenerated. The CM-6 suite `codemode-triage` grants them and adds the task `guest-of-a-name`, a DNS to lease to guest join over two hypervisor nodes, in both modes: MCP mode 6 turns, Code Mode 3, 4 broker calls each. The report: MCP mode 8/8 in 33 turns (mean 4.12), Code Mode 6/6 in 19 (mean 3.17); `codemode-triage` Code Mode wins 2, ties 3, loses 1, adoptable on the fakes. The golden harness now keeps one screener per conversation for the fenced tool results (`render_prompt` and `assemble_pack` take an optional `screener`; a fresh one per render remains the default), so a redacted IP address keeps its label across a run's results as the screener's contract says, and MCP mode joins DNS to DHCP on that label; the task's lease hostnames match no other name, so the label is the only join.
- The Code Mode runner reports the requests its relay refused (CM-11 of `docs/codemode.md`). A request of the program's own (presenting the run's token) that the relay refuses before the broker (an action that is no action name, a target that is no `svc:` id, a call after the run paused or stopped, past the step bound, a replay mismatch) never reached the broker and is no entry of `calls`, so it was in no part of the result; it is now listed in the execution result's `relay_refusals` (`schemas/codemode/execution_result`, draft: `at_step`, `op`, the `action` as sent, one line cut to 128 characters, `outcome` and `reason`; the first 64, the rest counted in `relay_refusals_omitted`; an example and three negatives), a list of its own because a `calls` entry is a step with a well-formed action and the broker's own outcome. Lines without the token are never listed, and a token that is not ASCII is such a line (it used to drop the connection uncounted); a refusal reason the runner does not name is `runner-fault`, logged. The `codemode-runner` contract gains rule i and the MUST check `relay-refusals-are-reported`, which also holds the omitted count and that a tokenless line is never listed (mutants `relay-refusals-hidden` and `foreign-lines-listed`, each caught by it only). The CM-6 harness derives every Code Mode attempt from the result (`calls` and `relay_refusals`; a non-zero `relay_refusals_omitted` is an ungranted attempt, so the task fails closed), `Backend.execute` returns the result alone, a refused `codemode.execute` is an attempt rather than a broker call, and the fake runner's tap is gone; the report's numbers are unchanged, the naive control now caught through `relay_refusals`.
- Code Mode programs can call the generated stubs (CM-10 of `docs/codemode.md`, phase 2). Handler documents may declare `target: required | optional` (`schemas/broker/handler` 1.3, additive; baseline frozen, with an example and a negative): whether a request for the action names the `svc:` target it is about. The broker refuses a request without a target for an action that requires one (`target-required`, audited) and matches a target whole (a trailing newline is `invalid-target`); `hnx codemode gen` gives the method a `target` (keyword-only in `stubs.pyi`, a property of the argument object in `types.d.ts`, `target` in `search.json`) and refuses a parameter named `target` on such an action. Both example handlers declare it `required`. `gen` also writes a fourth file per profile, `prelude.py`: the runtime prelude that binds each namespace to `broker.call` of its action (the target as the request's target, every other keyword as a param), so `service.status(target="svc:queue")` is exactly the broker request `broker.call` would send; `broker.call` keeps working. The `codemode-executor` contract's `run` takes an optional `prelude` (rule h: run first, in the program's namespace, inside the sandbox; a failing one is a `sandbox` error), the forking fake runs it, and the new MUST check `stubs-reach-the-broker` (mutant `prelude-dropped`, caught by it only) holds an executor to it. The runner never reads `prelude.py`: `homelabnext.codemode.runner.load_preludes` rebuilds each prelude from the profile's search index (`render.prelude_from_index`, deriving each method from its action's own name), reading indexes through an `O_NOFOLLOW` descriptor and refusing one that is not a regular file, is group- or world-writable or belongs to another user, so a tampered prelude or index cannot point a stub at another action. A namespace of `broker`, `result`, `__builtins__` or a Python builtin is refused by the generator; a missing required `target`, or a `target=` given to an action that takes none, is a `TypeError` in the sandbox before anything is sent; `gen --check` flags any file in a profile directory the generator does not write. The executor check also holds that a failing prelude is a `sandbox` error before the program runs (mutant `prelude-failure-swallowed`), and the CM-6 golden scripts call the stubs.
